| D-#386 | 2026-07-29 | **The assignment roster-pass workspace is SUBJECT-scoped for every teacher, class teacher included — closing a gap left when that rule shipped for homework.** Owner report: a class teacher opening Assignments saw every subject's cards for the section (Bangla, English, Maths, Science, BGS), which homework had not done since 2026-07-19. That owner decision — "the checking queue is subject-scoped for EVERY teacher; a class teacher's oversight is for reconciling the day, not for collecting and marking another teacher's subject" — was applied to `homeworkOpenRecords` at the time, but `assignmentOpenRecords` was missed and kept the default `classTeacherOversight: true`, which returns `null` (unrestricted) for the section's class teacher and homework-confirmer delegate. Both assignment queries now pass `classTeacherOversight: false`, and `assignmentItemTallies` matches so the COUNTS cannot reveal a subject whose cards the caller may not open. Principal/Office/whole-school supervisors are unaffected — they short-circuit to unrestricted inside the helper before the flag is read. **The regression guard is new and deliberate:** `allowedSubjectCodesForSection` was already well tested, but nothing asserted that each RESOLVER opts out of the class-teacher shortcut — a property of the call site, which is exactly what was wrong for ten days. `trackerWorkspaceSubjectScope.test.ts` reads the resolver source and requires the flag on all four workspace queries (verified field-specific: only 2 of 14 query fields in assignment.ts match, so removing the flag fails the test). |
| D-#387 | 2026-07-29 | **The GlitchTip skip list matches on CONSTRUCTOR name, not `.name` — until now it silently failed for every domain error class in the codebase's dominant pattern.** Prod report: `ClassTestResultError("No results entered for this exam — nothing to submit")` paged the maintainer, despite `ClassTestResultError` being listed in `EXPECTED_ERROR_NAMES` precisely so it would not. Cause: `class XError extends Error {}` — an empty body — never assigns `this.name`, so instances inherit `Error.prototype.name === "Error"`. `isExpectedError` checked `e.name` against the registry (never matches the class name), then a message regex (authz phrasing only), then `constructor.name === "Error"` (false for a subclass) — so an empty-bodied domain error failed all three and was reported as a real fault. The mechanism was exactly backwards from intent: the ~263 ad-hoc `new Error("business denial")` throws WERE skipped, while the purpose-built classes were not. Only classes that happen to set `name` in a constructor (ForbiddenError and friends) ever worked, which is why six weeks passed unnoticed. **The unit test could not catch it** — it fabricated `new Error("x"); e.name = <registered name>`, i.e. it asserted the registry against objects shaped unlike anything the app throws. Fix: match `Object.getPrototypeOf(err).constructor.name` as well (safe — the server ships as unminified `tsc` output, and the plain-Error branch already depended on it); test with REAL instances, including the actual exported class. Registry audit found 31 domain error classes, 27 registered: `PrintRequestError` + `Ref11ValidationError` added (pure input validation), `DocxConvertError` ("LibreOffice produced no valid PDF" — the converter is broken) and `SentrySmokeError` deliberately left as faults. `expectedErrorRegistry.test.ts` now forces every error class to be a conscious call — registered, or named in an explicit fault list — because "add new classes here as modules grow" was a manual step with nothing enforcing it. |
| D-#388 | 2026-07-29 | **A class teacher SEES the whole section again, but other subjects arrive FOLDED and READ-ONLY — the boundary moves from the read query to the render.** Owner ask, superseding both the 2026-07-19 narrowing and D-#386 (which applied it to assignments hours earlier): "he should see his own subject items and rest should be put under something collapsible so that he can open and see if he want." Hiding other subjects at the SERVER left the section coordinator unable to answer "where does my section stand?" at all, and starved `SubjectFold` — which has done exactly the requested job since D-#306 — of any input. So the four workspace READ queries drop `classTeacherOversight: false`; own subjects render expanded, every other subject collapses to one `▸ বিষয় (n)` toggle. **Opening a folded subject shows a roster read-out (student · state) and NO lifecycle controls** (owner choice over full access): collecting and marking stay with the subject teacher, honouring what the 2026-07-19 ruling was protecting while granting the visibility it cost. This is safe to render because the write boundary is elsewhere and untouchable from the client — `assertCanWrite` → `canWrite` honours only `teaching`/`proxy` grants matching section AND subject, with no class-teacher or supervisory escape (D-#17), so every hidden control would have 403'd anyway; the read-only body is honesty about that, not the gate. **Class notes are deliberately NOT included**: that screen uses subject FILTER chips rather than a fold, so restoring its visibility without a fold would dump every subject flat — the opposite of the ask. It needs its own UI change first. The regression guard was inverted rather than deleted: it now pins the read rule AND asserts the write gate beside it, because its previous version asserted the exact opposite and a guard pinning the wrong invariant is worse than none. |
| D-#389 | 2026-07-29 | **The auto-issue sweep looks back over recent school days, and class notes join the fold posture of D-#388.** Two owner asks in one slice, both closing gaps found in the 2026-07-28 homework audit. **(a) Sweep lookback:** `sweepHomeworkAutoIssue` queried `dateGiven` = TODAY only and runs 12:00–17:00, so a class whose last declaration or attendance landed after 17:00 was never revisited and its items sat in `declared` forever — 6 of the 24 items purged on 2026-07-28 died exactly that way, with every gate passing at audit time. It now iterates the last `HW_AUTO_ISSUE_LOOKBACK_SCHOOL_DAYS` = 5 school days, NEWEST FIRST so today is still served before any tail work. Crucially each day is confirmed **as itself** — its own `dateKey`, its own attendance-backed roster, its own gates — so a recovered Sunday can never spawn records off Thursday's absentees; the window widens WHICH days are checked, never WHAT they are checked against. 5 school days ≈ a week's tail: long enough to rescue a Thursday-evening declaration, short enough never to resurrect a day the school has moved on from. The walk is bounded in calendar days so a long holiday cannot loop. **(b) Class notes:** `classNotesForDate` drops `classTeacherOversight: false`, and `DailyNoteScreen` collapses not-my-subject notes behind a per-slot ▸ toggle rather than a list-level `SubjectFold` — the screen is period-shaped, not list-shaped, so the fold belongs on the slot. A slot the caller does not teach offers NO publish control (the mutation is subject-scoped and would refuse); `taught === null` (admin, or a subject-group view) renders everything expanded, the same null semantics `SubjectFold` uses. Reading is not writing: publish scope is untouched. |
| D-#390 | 2026-07-30 | **The reprint history was silently dropping a quarter of its rows, and its class axis could only ever offer classes the class-test path had tagged.** Owner report off the live screen: Nursery missing from the chips, no teacher filter, no date filter, "not all files are coming". A read-only count against prod settled all four at once: 269 printed/delivered jobs collapse to **263 grouped documents**, but the page limit defaulted to 200 and `rows.slice(0, limit)` carried **no flag** — 63 documents were cut with nothing on screen to say so. PQ6.8 had made the 1000-doc SCAN cap honest and left the page limit silent, and at 306 total jobs that scan flag never fires, so the list read as complete. Default+max are now the scan cap; `truncated` + `totalRows` report a short page. The class axis was derived from the returned rows, so it could only ever offer classes some job already carried — and **244 of the 269 carried no class at all**, because only `ClassTestService` passes one: `NewPrintRequestScreen` never sent `classId`/`subject`/`sectionId`, so every teacher-filed upload landed as "no class, no subject" and Nursery (roster level -1) appeared nowhere. Fixed at both ends — the chips come from the ROSTER, so a class with zero prints stays selectable and answers "nothing printed for Nursery yet" honestly; and the request form now names class + subject, both **optional**, because an office notice legitimately belongs to no class and a mandatory field would push teachers into picking a wrong one. The 244 existing rows are deliberately **left untagged**: class is not reliably inferable from a title, and they are now reachable by teacher + date, which is what was actually asked for. New filters: a printed-on window applied to the JOBS *before* grouping (so "printed in June" means a print landed in June, not that the document's newest print did), server-side because it must reach past the page window as the year fills — with the `printedAt`/`requestedAt` fallback the grouping already uses, so a backfilled row cannot vanish from a dated view; and a requester filter, Office-only (a teacher's scope is already their own rows, so the argument is ignored rather than allowed to widen it), keyed on `requesterIds` rather than a display name two staff could share. One more bug fell out while reading the screen: the "no class" chip shared the ANY sentinel with "all", so it rendered permanently lit and filtered nothing — it has its own sentinel now. Only dates re-query; class/subject/purpose/teacher filter client-side over the now-complete window, with a pinned selection dropped when a narrowed window removes its option (an invisible filter hiding every row is worse than a reset chip). |
| D-#391 | 2026-07-30 | **The print history browses by the class a job PRINTS FOR, which is `copiesClassId` whenever the job has no class of its own.** D-#390 fixed the class chips one layer too high: the axis offered every roster class, but the rows behind it still answered "no class", so the owner picked Nursery and got nothing, picked Class 1 and got 1 of 263 documents. The cause is that `classId` is set by exactly one path (`ClassTestService`) — 25 of 269 live printed jobs — while 172 of the remaining 244 carry `copiesClassId`, the class whose PRESENT-student count sets the copy count (D-#294). That field is not a guess about the job: a teacher who asks for "one per student present in Nursery" has named Nursery as plainly as the class-test path does, so the history reads `classId ?? copiesClassId` as the class the row is FOR. Measured through the real service against live data: Nursery 0→7, KG 1→9, Class 1 1→27, Class 2 4→35, Class 3 4→37, Class 4 7→36, Class 5 8→43, and the "no class" bucket falls 238→72. **Precedence is deliberate and one-directional** — the job's own `classId` wins where both exist, because a class-1 sheet whose count follows class-3 attendance is a class-1 print; the server filter mirrors that exactly (the `copiesClassId` branch is guarded by `classId: null`), so such a job can never surface under class 3. The effective class joins the **grouping key**, keeping the PQ6.2 rationale intact now that the class is actually known: one sheet printed for two classes stays two rows. It is exposed as `PrintHistoryRow.classId`/`classLevel`, deliberately **separate** from `latest.classId` — the job field keeps its own meaning for the queue, and only the history row carries the derived one, so nothing downstream silently inherits a fallback. The **72-document remainder is still left untagged** (fixed-count uploads and links, no section either) and is reached by teacher + date: D-#390 declined to infer class from a title and that stands — this decision adds a field the job already carries, it does not start guessing. Two clauses now need their own `$or` (class, dates), so the query AND-s them explicitly; assigning `q.$or` twice would have dropped the first filter silently, which is the same class of bug as the truncation D-#390 just fixed. |
| D-#392 | 2026-07-30 | **The 72 prints that name no class get tagged by a PERSON, with a pre-fill read out of their own file names — never by inference.** Owner ask straight after D-#391 shipped: "how can we fix no class items and tag appropriately". Every automatic route was measured against the live rows first, and each one fails on its own terms: the **link** jobs (18) are `docs.google.com/document/d/…` ids that resolve to nothing we hold, and none of those URLs appears on any job that DOES name a class, so there is nothing to join to; **requester scope** cannot disambiguate a single row, because no teacher in the roster teaches only one class (the closest, Mahzabin Yasmin, teaches Class 3 + Nursery, and the owner account has no teaching grant at all while filing 24 of the 72); and the **file-name codes**, which do read on 42 of 72, name a class the requester does not teach for **12 of the 22 rows where that could be cross-checked** — coordination work and shifted grants, indistinguishable from a bad read without asking. So the code becomes a **suggestion** and a human confirms it: the tag control pre-fills class (and subject, on 20 rows) and shows `suggestionEvidence` — the exact filename it read — so the person confirming can see what it is trusting. A tagged row gets no suggestion at all, so nothing invites re-tagging a class somebody already settled. **The unit tagged is the DOCUMENT, not the print**: the row groups by source × class × subject × purpose (PQ6.2), so tagging only the representative job would push it into a row of its own and orphan the older prints — the mutation takes the row's whole `jobIds` list and refuses the whole group if any job in it is out of the caller's reach, because a half-tagged group is a silently split row. **A class-test job is refused outright**: its class belongs to the ClassTest it mirrors (PQ-5/CT-1), and the history is not a side door onto the exam record. Scope mirrors reprint (Office any, teacher own) and both sides are audited (before + after + byOffice) because a tag is a judgement, not a fact — a later reader needs to know what it replaced and who decided. Omit-leaves / null-clears rather than a truthy-only setter, so a wrong tag is correctable without a migration. Subject validates against `ROUTINE_SUBJECTS` (the request form's vocabulary, incl. ARABIC/ISLAM/QURAN) not the content plane's narrower `SUBJECTS`, which would have made ISLAM — a real value in this data — untaggable. One implementation trap worth recording: `_` is a regex word character, so `Eng` never matches inside `C1_Eng_Block02`, the dominant shape in these file names; both readers normalize separators before matching, and a test pins it. |
| D-#393 | 2026-07-30 | **The monthly progress report is a FROZEN, VERSIONED DOCUMENT — a released revision is immutable, and late data creates revision N+1 rather than editing what a family has already read.** Owner ask: a per-student monthly report generated at month end "based on available data", updated when the teacher enters the rest, released by the Principal/Office individually or in bulk, and re-released when it changes. The load-bearing ruling is that this is a document, not a live view: `MonthlyReport` keyed `(studentId, periodKey, academicYearId)` carries a `snapshot` of the computed numbers, the config that produced them and both comments, and moves `DRAFT → READY → RELEASED → SUPERSEDED`. New data raises a **new revision in DRAFT beside the released one**, and the guardian keeps seeing revision N until someone releases N+1; a `changeLog` diff tells the office WHY a re-release is being asked of them. This mirrors the CM-1 posture that a delivered comment is sealed and a correction is a new comment. A recompute that changes no REPORTED number must NOT raise a revision, or the office drowns in re-release prompts for noise. The numbers themselves are not new work — they are the SP-1..SP-4 student-profile reads (D-#357–#360) re-windowed to a calendar month plus cohort comparators, so the feature is one collection, one singleton config and one permission, with everything else derived at read time (D-#85). |
| D-#394 | 2026-07-30 | **Every metric carries its denominator, its coverage and an as-of stamp, and an incomplete report CANNOT be released by default.** The owner's own scenario — "on the 30th the class test and some hw and as may not be checked" — is the feature's dominant failure mode: without coverage, an unmarked pile reads as a decline and the generated comment states it confidently to a guardian. So each stream reports `settled ÷ total` (via the SP-1 `lifecycleBuckets` state sets; a class test is settled when marks are entered), the report carries `provisional` + `dataAsOf`, and **release is BLOCKED while any stream is below `coverageGatePct` (default 80)** — Principal-only override, with a reason, audited. Two denominator rulings ride with it: **school days are DERIVED** (distinct dateKeys on which the section has any attendance record), so holidays, closures and Ramadan need no separate calendar and cannot silently inflate an absence rate; and every submission rate uses **expected-while-present** (items issued on days the child was present), with the raw issued count shown beside it, never instead of it — a child absent six days must not be scored against work handed out while they were away. Attendance coverage is 100% by construction, and is stated as such rather than computed. |
| D-#395 | 2026-07-30 | **A trend is a RULE, not a delta: minimum sample in BOTH months, then a threshold band, with "not comparable" as a first-class fourth state — and the thresholds are Principal-tunable but FROZEN into each revision.** Month-over-month raw deltas would manufacture trends out of a short month, a Ramadan schedule, or a subject with two homeworks. States resolve in order: `NOT_COMPARABLE` (either month below the min sample) → `DOWN` → `UP` → `STEADY`. Defaults: attendance ±5 pp / 10 school days; HW and AS submission ±10 pp / 5 and 3 items; quality ±10 pp / 5 checked; class test ±5 pp / 2 tests; concerns and resubmissions ±2 as raw counts. Three ABSOLUTE flags bypass trend entirely because a run reads worse than a total and a serious matter is not a rate: `absentStreakMax ≥ 3`, `absentUncovered ≥ 3`, any `SERIOUS_MATTER` concern. `MonthlyReportConfig` follows the `ObservationEscalationConfig` pattern exactly — `key: "SINGLETON"`, **read-time defaults, never seeded by a startup or bulk write against the shared live DB (D-#97)**. **The config used is stored in the snapshot**: without that, a threshold edited in September silently re-explains a July report a family has already read, and the chip on the page can no longer be reproduced. The same thresholds drive the rule-based section chips AND are printed in the sheet's appendix, so page and prose can never disagree. |
| D-#396 | 2026-07-30 | **Cohort comparison is by NUMBER ONLY — class average and class best, plus a school-best attendance figure — and it is suppressed in sections too small to hide a person.** Owner ruling: keep both class average and class best, and for attendance also "in the whole school what was the highest present count, without mentioning name, only number". So each rate carries `classAvg` + `classBest` over the student's SECTION, attendance additionally carries `schoolBestDays` over all active students, and **no name, roll or identifier of any other child appears anywhere in the report or in the AI facts JSON**. `classBest` is withheld (average only) when the section roster is below `minSectionSizeForClassBest` (default 5) — in a section of six, "the best" IS a person, and a report that leaves a family able to name the top child has become a ranking sheet, which is what the guardian-portal posture (D-#277) has refused from the start. Every rate also carries the student's **own best month this academic year**, so a weak child is measured against themselves as well as against the room. School-wide comparison applies to ATTENDANCE ONLY — it was the only one asked for, and extending it to marks would rank the school. |
| D-#397 | 2026-07-30 | **Office and Principal both release; only the Principal can override a block, revoke, or unlock — and a bulk release is one revocable batch.** Owner ruling that the office should release too. Both roles release and re-release, individually or over a section/class/whole-school selection that shows a count and a per-student exclusion list before it fires. A bulk release writes **one `releaseBatchId`**, so the first month's inevitable wrong bulk release is undone as a batch rather than student by student. Three powers stay Principal-only so a mistake has exactly one owner: overriding the D-#394 coverage block, revoking an already-released report (guardian access is withdrawn), and unlocking a report after the hard lock. All five actions write an audit row (ADR-008), because a release is a judgement about what a family is told. New permission **`report:release`** (Principal + Office) — a two-place contract sync: `/shared/vocab.ts` + the role→permission map + the harness, then the vocab verifier. **Reading needs no new permission**: staff ride the existing `assertReportRead` + the SP-1 two-tier subject gate, guardians ride the existing guardian gate and see RELEASED revisions of their own child only. |
| D-#398 | 2026-07-30 | **The revision window is 21 days and recompute is an idempotent external trigger, not a write hook.** Cadence: revision 1 auto-generated on the 1st; target release ~5th; nightly recompute through day 14; manual-only 15–20; **hard lock on day 21**, after which corrections belong to the NEXT month's report and reopening is a Principal act with a reason. 21 days keeps at most one month open at a time — a report re-released five weeks later confuses a family more than it corrects them. Mechanism: a nightly **idempotent `/triggers` endpoint under shared-secret auth (the AT-4 / D-#65 pattern)**, deliberately NOT an in-process cron (the notification ticker is already the single-instance timer, D-#73) and deliberately NOT a hook on every tracker mutation — a mark entered for 30 students would otherwise fire 30 recomputes of the same class. The trigger recomputes open reports for the closed month, diffs, and raises a revision only on a changed reported number (D-#393). |

Prompt rules pinned: Bangla, 2–4 sentences, exactly one action for home, never compare to a named peer, never diagnose, and **never narrate a `SERIOUS_MATTER`** — that routes to a human meeting. Every draft stores model + promptVersion + promptHash so a bad batch is traceable. |

Capacity is explicitly **not** a factor in this decision: images live in Drive, so Mongo holds JSON only — 764 KB for the whole of C1-BAN — and the choice is about isolation and ops. What is NOT given up is the original posture's real benefit: one action writes the complete folder — `book.json`, `images-compliant/`, `placements.json`, `layout.json` — as a zip that builds with the standalone CLI on any laptop. | D-#407 | 2026-07-31 | **The renderer is VENDORED AND SPAWNED, never ported — and it runs in a separate process from the school API.** `studybook-pipeline` is copied into `/book-pipeline/` as its own npm workspace (commonjs, puppeteer + sharp, the four Noto TTFs) and invoked with `execFile`/`shell:false`, exactly the `docxConvert` LibreOffice precedent already in this repo. The process split is the second half: Chromium is hundreds of MB per render and a 54-lesson book is minutes of work, so a `BookBuildJob` is claimed by a worker rather than blocking a GraphQL mutation, and an OOM in a book render must not take down live attendance and homework. Whether that worker shares the VM is left open (prd §8) — the host already runs prod, dev, Caddy and LibreOffice, and this adds the largest new memory consumer yet. |
| D-#408 | 2026-07-31 | **Both authoring paths emit the SAME patch object and pass the SAME validator before merge; the validator is the gate and the prompt is never the gate.** A chapter may be written in Claude Desktop and uploaded as a patch file, or generated by the in-app chat over the API — and downstream of the emit there is exactly one code path: validate against the ported checks, refuse the merge on any RED, replace the lesson wholesale on green. The compliance-strip chain is held back for a harder reason than convenience: it needs `placements.json` and the interactive `preview.js` placement editor, which is a whole second UI, and it is the least valuable part to move while being the fiddliest. **The white compliance stripe is applied programmatically post-generation and is NEVER written into a prompt** (README §5) — so the illustrator's screen shows `scene_description`, class, action and refs, and never `compliance_note`'s stripe language. Storage reuses the GP-A Drive store rather than growing a twin: `SCD-Hub-Files/<year>/books/<BOOK_ID>/<stage>/<lessonNo>-<slotId>-<n>.png` plus Drive `appProperties` so a file found from the Drive side identifies itself, with `BookImageAsset` as the index and `driveFileId` never leaving the server. | D-#410 | 2026-07-31 | **An escalation is anchored to an ITEM, runs many rounds, and its resolution NEVER mutates content — it produces a patch that cites it.** The reviewer↔senior-reviewer chain the owner described is back-and-forth over a specific thing (this block's narration, that slot's dress), so `BookEscalation` carries `{target ∈ LESSON|BLOCK|IMAGE_SLOT, targetId}` with an ordered message list and Drive-backed attachments; a senior's reply moves OPEN→ANSWERED and a further reply moves it back, indefinitely. It is its own model rather than a `ReviewAssignment` extension (that is keyed to `ContentArtifact` and belongs to plan review) and rather than a chat conversation (chat is conversation-centric and has no item to anchor to, no state, and no resolution). The load-bearing clause is the last one: **a resolution changes no lesson field.** The senior writes the answer; the author then submits a patch citing `escalationIds[]`, which passes the same validator as any other. That keeps exactly one write path into a lesson, and makes the ruling and its application two separately visible events — which is the difference between a log that records a decision and a log that records only its effect. A lesson cannot reach CONTENT_APPROVED with an escalation still OPEN. |
| D-#411 | 2026-07-31 | **Two logs, different jobs: `Audit` keeps answering "who did what"; a book-plane `BookEvent` answers "why does this sentence read this way".** The owner's requirement — every message and every reason for changing text or an image retrievable later in a dashboard — is an EDITORIAL record, not a security one. Merging it into the ADR-008 audit log would put reviewer prose and senior-reviewer rulings into the log the Principal reads for access oversight, and would also put book rows on the identity connection, undoing D-#404. Neither log is derivable from the other. | D-#412 | 2026-07-31 | **The in-app LLM authoring chat is built LAST, and it emits a schema-constrained patch rather than prose.** Ordering first: SB-1 foundations → SB-2 images → SB-3 review/escalation → SB-4 assembly → SB-5 rationale → SB-6 chat. The chat is the piece with the highest cost, the only new outbound AI dependency, and the largest unknown — and the Claude Desktop path **already works today**, so it is a convenience, not a dependency of anything above it. | D-#413 | 2026-07-31 | **Both the book database and the render worker live on the EXISTING VM — measured, not assumed — and the three constraints that makes load-bearing are pinned now.** Owner ruling ("book lives in vm db") plus a read-only capacity check of the live host on 2026-07-31. The numbers close prd §8 Q2 and Q3 outright: 4 OCPU / 23 GB with **1.4 GB in use and load average 0.00 after 48 days uptime**, 86 GB free of a 96 GB disk, both app servers together holding ~300 MB and GlitchTip's stack the rest. Steady-state addition is ~350 MB (a capped `mongod` + the worker); a render peaks at 2–4 GB for a few minutes against 22 GB available. **A second VM is explicitly refused**: this instance already consumes the whole Always Free ARM allocation (4 OCPU / 24 GB), so a second one bills on the PAYG-upgraded account — and would do so to relieve a host that is 94 % idle. Free-tier ceilings are not in play: 96 GB of the 200 GB block-storage limit, and PDF egress (Drive→server→client, since `driveFileId` never reaches a client) is nowhere near the 10 TB/month allowance. Three constraints ride with the ruling and are the reason it is a decision rather than an observation. **(a) `mongod`'s WiredTiger cache MUST be pinned to 1 GB** — the default is 50 % of (RAM − 1 GB), so an unconfigured install claims ~11 GB for a database holding under 100 MB of JSON and then contends with Chromium for it; backups ride the existing nightly cron rather than a second mechanism. **(b) The host is `aarch64` and Puppeteer publishes no bundled Chromium for linux-arm64** — so `npm install puppeteer` yields a library with nothing to launch. The fix is the OS Chromium (`chromium-browser`, a 200 MB snap, the only form Ubuntu 24.04 ARM offers) plus **`PUPPETEER_EXECUTABLE_PATH`**: an env var, which is precisely why it is the acceptable fix — the vendored renderer stays byte-identical and D-#407 holds. Launch must be verified under the systemd unit, not only an interactive shell, because snap confinement is where this bites. **(c) Build concurrency is 1**, because two simultaneous Chromiums are the only realistic way to pressure this host and **the VM carries no swap** — under real pressure the kernel OOM-kills rather than degrades. Already present and not to be reinstalled: `pdffonts` (poppler-utils) for the post-render font audit and `soffice` from the docxConvert precedent; `sharp` has proper arm64 prebuilds. Only Chromium needs installing. |
| D-#414 | 2026-08-01 | **The Principal gets a free-tier headroom panel, and measuring the three ceilings first changed what it shows.** Owner ask: see the VM and MongoDB status in time to act. Rather than assume, all three were read live before designing — and the assumption was wrong in both directions. **Google Drive, which holds every uploaded byte** (`StoredFile` is only a handle, D-#70) and looked like the obvious risk, sits at **1.33 TB of a 100 TB pooled Workspace quota**: not a constraint at all, so file growth does not threaten anything. **Atlas M0 is the ceiling that binds**, at 76.2 MB of 512 MB — and the decisive detail is that **the cap is per CLUSTER, not per database**: the cluster carries `scdhub_prod` (41.7 MB) alongside `scdhub_local` (18.2 MB) and `scdhub_dev` (16.4 MB), so the **test copies eat ~45% of current usage — production's own headroom**. A per-database panel, the obvious build, would have hidden exactly the fact worth acting on; the read is therefore cluster-wide and lists every database with the current one marked, and it degrades to "this database only — real usage is higher" rather than presenting a partial total as complete when `listDatabases` is denied. The Atlas limit is a **documented constant** because no command reports a cluster's plan limit — honest config beats a bar that silently drifts after an upgrade. **Egress needed a design, not a reading:** Linux exposes only counters SINCE BOOT, so a single sample can never answer "how much this month". A daily `NetSnapshot` (captured by the existing notification ticker — no new scheduler, D-#73 — placed ABOVE the school-day gate, since traffic does not stop on a holiday) turns the counter into deltas; a reading LOWER than the previous day is the unambiguous signature of a reboot, so that day contributes its raw counter and the month is flagged **partial**, because the traffic between the last snapshot and the reboot is genuinely unrecoverable and pretending otherwise would overstate the remaining allowance. One snapshot yields null, not zero — a single reading is not a delta. **Bands at 70% amber / 85% red**, early enough that pruning is a choice rather than an emergency; a missing or zero limit yields "unknown", never a false all-clear. **Every section carries its own error and the query still answers** — a health screen that 500s when one probe dies hides the numbers that were fine, which is the opposite of its job. Gated on `audit:read` (Principal-only) with **no new permission**, per the D-#281 precedent: a new one would drag a read-only panel through the two-place contract sync. **Deliberately read-only** — no prune button. Deleting school data from a dashboard is a different decision with different safety requirements, and the panel's job is to make the case for it, not to execute it. Privacy: counters only, no identity document read, and the payload excludes the connection string, every credential, and the storing Google account's address. |
| D-#415 | 2026-08-01 | **The monthly comment gets a SECOND LANE — export to Markdown, author in Claude Desktop, import validated JSON — and it is held to exactly the same guards as the in-app model.** Owner ask, after the in-app Gemini lane shipped: the free tier rate-limits mid-class, the paragraph is serviceable rather than good, and the school already authors curriculum this way (`docs/import-workflow.md`), so this is that established workflow pointed at comments rather than a new one invented. Both lanes write `commentDraft`; nothing downstream can tell them apart. **The export is DE-IDENTIFIED (D-#399 applies MORE here, not less** — a file pasted into a chat window is a wider exposure than an API call): blocks are keyed by `reportId` and carry the same whitelisted facts `commentFactsOf` builds, with no name, roll or phone, and the app re-attaches identity on the way back. The instruction block is the SAME text as the MR-4 prompt, kept in one place so the two lanes cannot drift apart. **The return format is JSON, not Markdown** — parsing prose breaks on the first stray heading — and the envelope is validated on arrival, naming every bad row rather than dropping it. **Every guard runs again on import** (`validateNumerals` against that report's own facts, `looksLikeProse`, and a refusal on a RELEASED/SUPERSEDED revision), because a human having pasted the text does not make its numbers real. **The export is BOUND TO THE REVISION** via `revision` + a hash of `reportedFigures`: if a mark landed between export and import the comment describes numbers nobody will see, so it is refused with the remedy stated — otherwise this feature quietly reintroduces exactly the drift D-#393's freeze rule exists to prevent. **An imported comment is still a DRAFT** (owner ruling): a person presses accept in the app, so the one rule holding across both lanes is that nothing is released that a person has not accepted IN THE APP — treating the upload as the review would let a file satisfy the release gate. Scope is per-section AND whole-school (owner: "should have both option"), the section file being ~21 children and a manageable paste. |

| D-#416 | 2026-08-01 | **The health panel gains a trend, a projection, a heartbeat, a prune list and a backup — and the history is reconstructed rather than waited for.** Owner ask straight after D-#414 shipped: charts, and the ability to see earlier data. The obvious answer was "start snapshotting and come back in a month"; the better one came from checking what the data already knows. **Every large collection carries a creation time** (`createdAt`/`eventAt`, and `_id` embeds one regardless), so document counts can be walked backwards exactly — the chart opens with ~7 weeks of real curve instead of a single dot. **Bytes cannot be reconstructed** (Mongo keeps no history of collection sizes), so a backfilled day is priced at `count × today's bytes-per-document`, flagged `estimated`, drawn in muted ink AND captioned in words: colour alone must never be what separates a measured point from a derived one. **The bug that only running it could find:** the first live run produced a series jumping 9 MB → 76 MB on the day of the deploy, because a reconstructed day can only sum the tracked collections of ONE database while a measured day reports the whole-CLUSTER total that the cap counts. Two different quantities on one axis, an eight-fold cliff, and a projection fitted across the join. Fixed by adding a **constant baseline** for what the walk cannot see (other databases, the untracked tail) — which puts every point in the unit of the limit, and whose assumption (the unseen part was flat) is precisely why those rows stay flagged. **The projection refuses to guess**: under three points is not a trend, a flat or shrinking series has no crossing date, past-the-limit reports zero rather than a negative, and a null day is a gap rather than a zero — a confident wrong date is worse than an admission here, because it is the line the Principal would actually plan against. **SH-5 heartbeat:** a stalled ticker silently stops homework auto-DUE/auto-ISSUE, attendance reminders, class-note prompts, library sweeps and every escalation, so it is now on the panel with the consequence spelled out in words rather than left to a red chip; the heartbeat state moved to its own leaf module because the health service must report it while the scheduler imports the health service — a cycle TypeScript compiles happily and Node resolves to `undefined` by load order. **SH-6 prunable:** an ALLOWLIST, never "everything old" — `audits` is excluded by rule (ADR-008 append-only) and school records are excluded because they are the product, not exhaust; both pinned by tests. **Report only, no prune button**: deleting school data from a dashboard is a different decision with different safety requirements. **SH-7 backup:** M0 has no automated backups, so the honest headline is that "off" means no restore point exists — a weekly streamed gzip-NDJSON dump to the school's own Drive, no `mongodump` to provision, retention of four that touches only files this job created and runs only AFTER a successful upload so a failure never costs an existing restore point, `ageDays` measured from the last SUCCESS so a nightly failure cannot look fresh, and failures recorded because a page showing only successes is how a broken backup goes unnoticed. It ships **OFF behind `BACKUP_ENABLED=1`**: a job that writes to Drive on a schedule starts when a person decides, not when a deploy lands. |
| D-#417 | 2026-08-01 | **Per-slot LINEAGE with staleness invalidation is adopted into the support-book module, taken from the storybook workbench where it is proven over five completed books.** Reviewing `C:\Users\HP\Downloads\Story Book` surfaced a working internal production app (`workbench/`, July 2026) built over the same renderer, and its best idea was one the support-book PRD did not have at all. Each image slot carries `lineage: { approved, cropped, upscaled, compliant }` — a fingerprint of each stage's output — and **re-approving a slot's image marks every downstream artifact for that slot STALE**, with **any STALE artifact anywhere locking assembly** until cleared. The UI names the stale FILES, not a generic "out of date" stage. The reason this is a decision and not a nicety: the failure it prevents is silent. A re-approved image whose compliant version was never regenerated still builds a perfectly valid PDF — it just prints the old picture. With one person and one book that is a mistake you catch; with 201 image slots across 54 lessons and five people, it is the default outcome of a busy week. The workbench's companion rule comes too: `LINEAGE_STATES = [FRESH, STALE, MISSING]`, and re-running a stage is idempotent so "run stale only" and "run all" are always both safe. |
| D-#418 | 2026-08-01 | **Human review gates are REAL gates that the system may never satisfy on a person's behalf, and the build streams its log live.** Second adoption from the workbench. `REVIEW_GATES` (image grid reviewed, crop grid, upscale texture, strip grid, render spot-check) each store `{by, at}`; the app never auto-advances past one and **never infers a human judgement from a green exit code**. The workbench states this as "the app never sets `anchor.verified` — it displays it, a human ticks it upstream", and the identical principle protects `reviewer_signoff.checklist_passed` here: a validator proves the text is well-formed, never that a person looked at it. Movement rules mirror the workbench's stage machine — **backward is always allowed** (re-open images, re-pick a placement), forward past an unmet gate never is. Alongside it, the render worker streams the spawned script's stdout/stderr to the browser over **SSE** (`text/event-stream`, one-directional, no WebSocket — the workbench's proven choice) and writes the full log beside the job, because a build that fails on lesson 31's fit guard should say so **while it is failing**: the person watching is the one who can fix it, and a four-minute silence followed by a report wastes the only window where that is cheap. |
| D-#419 | 2026-08-01 | **Images get TWO permanent paths — external upload and in-app API — and neither is a migration away from the other. Amends D-#409.** Owner ruling: "keep both because currently will generate using gpt desktop later may integrate api." D-#409 had ruled image generation OUT of the app on the reasoning that the first book was made that way; the storybook workbench disproves the premise, since it already generates in-app against a model with cast refs attached and per-slot iteration threads, over five books. So `EXTERNAL_UPLOAD` ships in SB-2 and stays **forever** — a person with a better result from a tool the app does not call must always be able to bring it in — and `IN_APP_API` lands as SB-7. The structure is deliberately D-#408's: **downstream of the upload there is exactly one code path** — same `BookImageAsset`, same slot states, same lineage, same reviewer view — and the originating path is recorded in `IMAGE_SOURCES` for the timeline and branched on nowhere else. Two carry-overs from the workbench are non-negotiable in the API path: the **cast reference sheet is attached to every generation of a canon character, never generated from text alone** (the drift-prevention rule — small drift compounds across a book), and every attempt is retained, not just the winner. The stripe stays programmatic and post-generation; **no stripe language ever enters a prompt**, on either path. |
| D-#420 | 2026-08-01 | **The storybook line is re-scoped as SCHOOL-FIRST, which makes it eligible to share this module — so the SB-1..SB-7 core is built BOOK-TYPE-AGNOSTIC from the start.** Owner ruling: "though it was designed for commercial use but now we will use it for school primarily." That matters because the blocker was never technical — the two products share a renderer, an image chain and a production shape — it was the upstream absolute in README §7 that support books never enter a sales catalog. With both lines school-first that rule's PURPOSE is satisfied rather than violated, and the commercial framing is **demoted, not deleted**: nothing here forecloses selling later, but no storefront, reader, pricing or payment surface enters this repo without its own PRD and its own ruling. The consequence is a design constraint on SB-1 rather than extra work in it: `SupportBook` becomes one `bookType` among others, and the stage machine, lineage, review gates, job runner, escalation chain and rationale timeline carry no সহায়িকা-specific assumptions, with type-specific pieces (schema, validator checks, render profiles, policy set) behind a per-type adapter — the same shape ASSEMBLY §1 already uses to keep a shared renderer neutral. Deliberately NOT decided: whether `workbench/` is migrated, rewritten or left running on the laptop, and the storybook's own slice set. **The workbench is producing books today and nothing in this module may stop it** — five S1 titles are assembled through it, `GB-B01` is mid-flight, and a `chapter-book-48` format was specified 2026-08-01 that must land additively (`S1-B01` must still build byte-identically). |
| D-#421 | 2026-08-01 | **The storybook IS hosted in this system, as a first-class second `bookType` on the same engine — superseding D-#420's "not decided" clause.** Owner ruling: "update prd to host the storybook in the system like study book." D-#420 had already made the engine book-type-agnostic and left the storybook's own home open; this closes it. Build contract: `docs/prd-storybook.md`, slices ST-1..ST-5. **It is a second type, not a second module** — not a new plane, not a new database, and notably **not a single new permission**: the seven `book:*` grants (D-#405) cover author, illustrator, reviewer, senior reviewer and assembler identically for both lines, because the two differ in *content rules*, not in *process shape*. Both are: author a JSON → generate images against reference sheets → apply a compliance strip → review → render → log why. What the per-type adapter supplies is exactly four things — the JSON schema, the validator's check set, the render profiles (six outputs for a storybook against the সহায়িকা's two), and the policy doc set — and **nothing else in the engine may branch on `bookType`**. One genuinely new gate rides with it (ST-3): the SOP's anchor verification is a *separation* rule — a different chat and preferably a different model than the one that wrote the story, then a human confirming the hadith reference personally — so `ANCHOR_STATES` has four values with `LLM_CHECKED` deliberately **not** a pass ("an LLM 'verified' is a lead, not a verdict", SOP 2.3), the verifier may not be the book's author (the observer≠observed guard shape), and assembly is locked on anything but `HUMAN_VERIFIED`. The commercial exclusion from D-#420 is **permanent until its own PRD**: no storefront, reader, DRM, pricing, subscription or payment surface enters this repo, and nothing in the storybook contract prepares one. Cutover is the one thing still open — `GB-B01` is mid-flight and `S4-B01` importing on the workbench, which keeps running and is replaced function by function, never in one cut. |
| D-#422 | 2026-08-01 | **Upscayl is dropped; upscaling is sharp on both pipelines.** Owner ruling after the constraint was measured. The storybook workbench's upscale stage shells out to `C:\Program Files\Upscayl\resources\bin\upscayl-bin.exe` — a **Windows executable**, a Vulkan/NCNN GPU upscaler with no ARM64 Linux build, on a host that is Neoverse-N1 with only a virtio render node and no usable GPU. It is the single piece of the storybook chain that could not move to the server at all. Rather than keep a laptop in the middle of a server pipeline, the step moves to **sharp** — which `studybook-pipeline` already uses, which has proper linux-arm64 prebuilds, and which therefore leaves **both pipelines sharing one server-runnable upscale step instead of two incompatible ones**. The quality question is settled by the existing gate rather than by preference: output must clear the pipeline's own minimum-dimension check (≥2500 px for storybooks, the DPI floor for সহায়িকা), and **that gate is the arbiter, not the tool** — if sharp output fails it, the failure is loud at validate time, not silent at print. Accepted trade-off: an AI upscaler and a Lanczos resample are not the same thing, and a visible drop on real artwork would be grounds to revisit — checked against a real book at ST-4, not assumed. |
| D-#423 | 2026-08-01 | **Host additions for the second pipeline are exactly one package, because the expensive things are already shared.** Measured on the live VM 2026-08-01, not assumed: **Python 3.12.3 is present but Pillow is NOT** (`ModuleNotFoundError: No module named 'PIL'`), and the `pipeline-tools` scripts — `crop_edges.py`, `apply_strips.py`, `make_strips.py` — need it, so `python3-pil` is the one genuine install the storybook adds. Everything else is already accounted for: **one Chromium serves both renderers** (no second browser, no second 200 MB snap), `pdffonts` and `soffice` are installed, `sharp` has arm64 prebuilds and now covers upscaling for both lines (D-#422), and **builds stay serialized at concurrency 1** — so a second book type adds **no memory peak at all**, only about 1 GB of disk against 86 GB free. This is why hosting both was a cheap decision rather than a capacity one: the costly resources are per-*build*, not per-*pipeline*, and there is only ever one build running. |
| D-#424 | 2026-08-01 | **The Principal holds all seven `book:*` permissions by template, and every same-person separation becomes a STAMP rather than a WALL for that one role.** Owner ruling: "principal can do everything, author, review etc." The permission half is trivial and was already reachable through AC-1 — it is now simply the template, so nothing has to be granted per book. The half that needed a decision is that **permissions were never the constraint**: the separations in these contracts are **row-level** — a reviewer is not the author of *that* lesson (SB-3), a verifier is not the author of *that* book (ST-3, the SOP's fresh-eyes rule) — and holding every grant does not by itself dissolve them. Three resolutions were possible: keep refusing (blocks a one-person operation entirely), silently allow (a sign-off that reads as a second pair of eyes when it was the same pair — the worst option, because the record becomes a lie), or **allow and record**. Allow-and-record wins: `selfReviewed` / `selfVerified` is stamped automatically when author and reviewer are the same person, shown wherever the sign-off is shown, and carried in the rationale timeline. **The rule's purpose was never to obstruct the Principal — it was so a later reader can tell whether a second pair of eyes saw the work**, and a stamp answers that honestly where a refusal answers it not at all. **What does NOT change:** `HUMAN_VERIFIED` still requires a named human and a timestamp — self-verification changes *who may*, never *whether a human did*, so no automated or model output can set it (D-#418 holds); assembly still locks on an unverified anchor; and the separation stays **enforced for every non-Principal role**, since the exception exists for the school's staffing reality, not as a general loosening. This follows the repo's established override posture (`ContentArtifact.approvalOverride` + `approvalNote`, D-#394's Principal-only coverage override) with one deliberate difference: **no typed reason is required**, because for a solo Principal it would read "nobody else available" on every single book, which the flag already states. |
| D-#425 | 2026-08-01 | **Two corrections the owner caught, both from me trusting an API over the repo and over the screen a person actually looks at.** **(a) The backup job of D-#416 was pure duplication and is deleted.** It was built on "Atlas M0 has no automated backups, so no restore point exists" — true of Atlas, false of this school: `scripts/backup.sh` has run nightly from cron since 2026-06-30 (ADR-011), dumping prod with `mongodump --archive --gzip` into the Drive folder `SCD-Hub-Backups`, with a restore runbook that was **tested by doing** (DEP-4 drill, restored into `scdhub_dev`). All of it was in this repo the whole time; I looked in Drive only after the owner showed me the folder. SH-7 is therefore rewritten as a **monitor**: it lists that folder and reports the newest archive's age, read-only — it never writes, uploads or deletes, because a monitor that can delete its own subject is not a monitor. **The band must come from the newest archive's age ALONE, never from the spacing between files:** `drive-backup.mjs` rotates grandfather-father-son (7 daily / 4 weekly / 3 monthly), so the folder legitimately looks weekly beyond the last 7 days. I misread exactly that thinning as "the job missed nights" before checking the rotation — the same mistake the monitor would make every week if it scored gaps. A missing folder and an empty folder are both red (either way there is no restore point) and it is never auto-created, because a missing folder is a finding and conjuring an empty one would hide it; an unreachable Drive is "unknown", never "no backups", since reporting a disaster on a failed network call sends someone hunting a problem that is not happening. **(b) The Drive card was understating the ceiling by ~40x.** It read `storageQuota.usage` (1.33 TB, spanning every Google service) against `storageQuota.limit` (109951162777600 = 100 TiB, the sentinel Google returns for a pooled Workspace allowance) and rendered **1.3% "Healthy"** — while the account's own Drive page shows **51.57 GB of 100 GB**. The card now takes **`usageInDrive`** against a **configured** limit (`DRIVE_LIMIT_GB`, default 100 GB): the pair the page shows is the pair a person can check, and a headroom panel whose whole purpose is early warning must never be the thing that hides a ceiling. Live position is 51.6% — Drive is the **second-tightest** limit, not the non-issue D-#414 recorded. `usageInDriveTrash` is surfaced separately as **reclaimable**: 11.61 GB, about a fifth of usage, still counting against the quota until the bin is emptied. **The lesson worth keeping:** both errors came from believing a remote API's self-description over cheaper, closer sources — the repo's own `scripts/`, and the screen the owner sees every day. Read those first. |

| D-#431 | 2026-08-01 | **The anchor gate is NOT APPLICABLE to a book that declares no anchor, and that state is DERIVED, never selectable.** Found in the field, not in review: GB-B01 (`chapter-book-48`, 45 pages of chapter-opener/prose-plate/prose, গুবলেট বিজ্ঞান — a Class 4 science title, correctly carrying no anchor page) could not be assembled through the workbench at all. Its gate reads `!!(book.anchor && book.anchor.verified === true)`, so an ABSENT anchor collapses to `false` and a book with nothing to verify was treated exactly like one with an **unverified narration** — locked forever, with the message "guide 5 forbids shipping an unverified narration" about a narration that does not exist. The book was only built by bypassing the app, which is why the workbench still reported `assembly: locked` while eight finished PDFs sat on disk. **The same defect was written into `prd-storybook.md` ST-3** ("assembly is locked unless HUMAN_VERIFIED") a day earlier and would have blocked every chapter book and every non-Islamic-values title identically. Fix in both places: `ANCHOR_STATES` gains `NOT_APPLICABLE`, and the gate passes on `HUMAN_VERIFIED` **or** `NOT_APPLICABLE`. **The derivation clause is the load-bearing half** — `NOT_APPLICABLE` comes from the book declaring no anchor and no anchor page, and a user may never set it, because a selectable "not applicable" is a one-click way to skip verification on a book that genuinely needs it, which is precisely the failure this gate exists to prevent. Applied to the live workbench (`server/books.js`, `server/importer.js`) with a tri-state `anchor_status` exposed so the UI says "no anchor in this book" rather than a green tick nobody earned; verified against the running server — GB-B01 now reports `not_applicable` and its lock reasons are down to the honest two (strips not done, 22 stale files). |
